The authors assess the culture of AI in offensive security research regarding ethics communication, highlighting trends, best practices, and gaps in the current discourse. The analysis reveals that most reviewed papers acknowledge the dual-use potential of their research and primarily justify it by arguing that it aids in improving security by helping defenders prepare against AI-guided attacks and by making penetration testing more accessible. The paper also discusses balancing the drive for innovation with ethical responsibility, touching on topics like sandboxing, disclosure practices, human-in-the-loop approaches, and transparency.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper addresses an important and timely issue: the ethical implications of using LLMs for offensive security research. As LLMs become more powerful and accessible, their potential misuse for malicious purposes becomes a growing concern. The paper's analysis of existing literature to understand how researchers are currently grappling with these ethical dilemmas is valuable.

Strengths:

*   **Timeliness and Relevance:** The topic is highly relevant given the rapid advancements in LLMs and their increasing application in cybersecurity.
*   **Systematic Analysis:** The paper employs a structured thematic analysis to assess the ethical considerations presented in the selected research papers.
*   **Practical Insights:** The identification of trends, best practices, and gaps in the current discourse provides practical guidance for researchers in the field.
*   **Clear Recommendations:** The paper offers specific recommendations for future research, such as including dedicated ethics sections, transparently stating motivations, and emphasizing responsible disclosure.

Weaknesses:

*   **Limited Scope:** The analysis is confined to a relatively small set of 15 prototypes, which might not be fully representative of the entire landscape of LLM-based offensive security research. The reliance on specific search strategies, including forward and backward referencing, might also introduce selection bias.
*   **Depth of Analysis:** While the paper identifies the presence or absence of ethical considerations, it could benefit from a more in-depth qualitative analysis of the justifications and arguments presented in each paper. Simply noting the existence of an ethics statement provides limited insight if the arguments provided within are weak or perfunctory.
*   **Lack of Comparative Analysis:** The paper could have included a comparative analysis between different research areas using offensive security tooling, i.e., cloud security or software security.
*   **Overly Optimistic Conclusion:** While the authors acknowledge the dual-use potential, the conclusion leans towards a more optimistic view, potentially downplaying the risks associated with releasing offensive security tools, even with mitigation strategies.

Novelty and Significance:

The paper's contribution lies in its systematic examination of the ethical considerations within LLM-based offensive security research. It provides a snapshot of the current state of awareness and practices in the field. However, the novelty is somewhat limited because the ethical challenges of dual-use technology are well-established in cybersecurity. The significance is moderate, as it raises awareness and offers guidance but doesn't propose entirely new frameworks or solutions for addressing these ethical concerns. The reliance on thematic analysis, while appropriate, limits its ability to offer groundbreaking insights. The impact of the paper will depend on whether the community actively adopts its recommendations and contributes to a more robust ethical discourse around LLM-based offensive security tools.

Score: 6

Justification:

A score of 6 reflects the paper's moderate contribution to the field. The paper addresses a relevant and timely topic with a systematic analysis, providing valuable insights and practical recommendations. However, the limited scope, depth of analysis, and reliance on existing ethical frameworks prevent it from achieving a higher score. While the paper provides a good overview of current practices, it doesn't offer groundbreaking solutions or significantly advance the understanding of ethical dilemmas in this area. The potential influence is present, but its realization depends on active community engagement and the emergence of more innovative approaches to addressing ethical concerns.

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 6/10

### Educators' Perceptions of Large Language Models as Tutors: Comparing Human and AI Tutors in a Blind Text-only Setting
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.08702v1)
- **Authors**: Sankalan Pal Chowdhury, Terry Jingchen Zhang, Donya Rooein, Dirk Hovy, Tanja Käser, Mrinmaya Sachan
- **Abstract**: The rapid development of Large Language Models (LLMs) opens up the possibility of using them as personal tutors. This has led to the development of several intelligent tutoring systems and learning assistants that use LLMs as back-ends with various degrees of engineering. In this study, we seek to compare human tutors with LLM tutors in terms of engagement, empathy, scaffolding, and conciseness. We ask human tutors to annotate and compare the performance of an LLM tutor with that of a human tutor in teaching grade-school math word problems on these qualities. We find that annotators with teaching experience perceive LLMs as showing higher performance than human tutors in all 4 metrics. The biggest advantage is in empathy, where 80% of our annotators prefer the LLM tutor more often than the human tutors. Our study paints a positive picture of LLMs as tutors and indicates that these models can be used to reduce the load on human teachers in the future.
- **Summary**: Here's a concise summary and a critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores educators' perceptions of Large Language Models (LLMs) as tutors, comparing them to human tutors in a blind, text-only setting.  Human annotators with teaching experience are tasked with comparing dialogue snippets between an LLM-based tutor (MWPTutor) and a human tutor (MathDial) across four metrics: engagement, empathy, scaffolding, and conciseness.  The results indicate that the annotators perceive LLMs as performing better than human tutors in all four metrics, with empathy showing the biggest difference. The authors provide a detailed analysis of the results, exploring potential reasons for the LLMs' perceived superiority and discussing implications for educators and learning scientists. The study also releases the annotation data for future research.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Relevant and Timely Research Question:** The comparison between LLM and human tutors is crucial given the increasing integration of LLMs into education.
*   **Well-Defined Metrics:** The paper establishes clear and scientifically grounded metrics (engagement, empathy, scaffolding, conciseness) that attempt to go beyond simply measuring learning gains.
*   **Blind Evaluation:** The blind pairwise comparison setup helps to mitigate biases, though not eliminate them entirely.
*   **Release of Annotation Data:**  Making the annotated dataset publicly available is a valuable contribution that allows for future research and validation of the study's findings.
*   **Detailed Analysis and Discussion:** The authors provide a thorough discussion of the results, including possible explanations for the observed differences and their implications. They critically discuss potential biases and limitations of the study.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Scope and Setting:** The study is limited to text-only interactions and a specific type of math problem (MWPs). This limits the generalizability of the findings to other domains and tutoring modalities. The fact that the LLM annotators were compared to human annotators working without real students present potentially introduces significant confounding factors.
*   **Potential LLM Biases:**  The use of LLMs to annotate the same metrics introduces potential bias, as LLMs are likely to favor their own generation styles and patterns. Though these biases were somewhat addressed through shuffling the ordering of the two systems in the prompt, it is not enough to resolve these issues.
*   **Annotation Bias:** Using only annotators with teaching experience might introduce biases depending on the type of teaching they were performing.
*   **MWPTutor and MathDial Differences:** It is unclear to what extent the differences observed were because of the tutor used. MWPTutor as mentioned in the paper is more focused on maintaining correctness, while MathDial aims to generate as human-like responses as possible. As such, the higher performance could be primarily driven due to the constraint that MWPTutor has to maintain correctness, instead of more innate factors.
*   **Subjectivity of Metrics:** The subjective nature of the metrics introduces potential for variability in the annotators' judgments and potentially compromises the reliability of the results. The metrics could have been more precisely defined to increase inter-rater reliability.
*   **Truncation:** While the authors note that truncating all dialogs helps with annotator burden, such truncation also potentially loses the ability to measure many elements of scaffolding, engagement, and other metrics, making the measures less precise.
* **Statistical Significance and Effect Size:** While the paper claims the results were significant for all metrics except for Engagement, the lack of a significant difference between MWPTutor and MathDial would suggest that there is still a large degree of overlap, especially given the relatively low kappa scores.

**Novelty and Significance:**

The novelty of the work lies in the direct comparison of LLM and human tutors based on pedagogical qualities, rather than just learning outcomes. This helps to better understand the strengths and weaknesses of LLMs as tutors.

The significance of the study is that it provides empirical evidence suggesting that LLMs can potentially be effective tutors, even exceeding human tutors in certain aspects (in this limited context). This has important implications for the future of education and the role of AI in supporting student learning. While preliminary, the study highlights the potential for delegating some tutoring responsibilities to AI to free up human teachers for more complex tasks.

**Score:** 6.5

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a timely and well-executed study that addresses an important question in education. The use of a blind evaluation setup and the release of the annotation data are positive aspects. However, the study is limited by its scope, setting, and potential biases in the evaluation process. The significant limitations with bias and subjectivity detract from the rigor of the results and their ability to generalize. While the findings are interesting and point to the potential of LLMs as tutors, more research is needed to validate these results in more realistic settings with real students and more diverse tutoring contexts. A score of 6.5 reflects the paper's contributions to the field, tempered by the identified limitations.

- **Classification**: cs.ET
- **Score**: 6/10

### PhyBlock: A Progressive Benchmark for Physical Understanding and Planning via 3D Block Assembly
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.08708v1)
- **Authors**: Liang Ma, Jiajun Wen, Min Lin, Rongtao Xu, Xiwen Liang, Bingqian Lin, Jun Ma, Yongxin Wang, Ziming Wei, Haokun Lin, Mingfei Han, Meng Cao, Bokui Chen, Ivan Laptev, Xiaodan Liang
- **Abstract**: While vision-language models (VLMs) have demonstrated promising capabilities in reasoning and planning for embodied agents, their ability to comprehend physical phenomena, particularly within structured 3D environments, remains severely limited. To close this gap, we introduce PhyBlock, a progressive benchmark designed to assess VLMs on physical understanding and planning through robotic 3D block assembly tasks. PhyBlock integrates a novel four-level cognitive hierarchy assembly task alongside targeted Visual Question Answering (VQA) samples, collectively aimed at evaluating progressive spatial reasoning and fundamental physical comprehension, including object properties, spatial relationships, and holistic scene understanding. PhyBlock includes 2600 block tasks (400 assembly tasks, 2200 VQA tasks) and evaluates models across three key dimensions: partial completion, failure diagnosis, and planning robustness. We benchmark 21 state-of-the-art VLMs, highlighting their strengths and limitations in physically grounded, multi-step planning. Our empirical findings indicate that the performance of VLMs exhibits pronounced limitations in high-level planning and reasoning capabilities, leading to a notable decline in performance for the growing complexity of the tasks. Error analysis reveals persistent difficulties in spatial orientation and dependency reasoning. Surprisingly, chain-of-thought prompting offers minimal improvements, suggesting spatial tasks heavily rely on intuitive model comprehension. We position PhyBlock as a unified testbed to advance embodied reasoning, bridging vision-language understanding and real-world physical problem-solving.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary and a critical evaluation of the "PhyBlock: A Progressive Benchmark for Physical Understanding and Planning via 3D Block Assembly" paper.

**Summary:**

The paper introduces PhyBlock, a new benchmark designed to evaluate vision-language models (VLMs) in their ability to understand and reason about physics, specifically in the context of 3D block assembly tasks. PhyBlock consists of two main components: a hierarchical assembly planning task and a physical-understanding VQA task. The assembly task features a four-level cognitive hierarchy, progressing from basic perception to advanced spatial planning. The VQA task is designed to measure models' explicit understanding of physical concepts like object properties, spatial relationships, and scene dynamics.  The authors benchmarked 21 state-of-the-art VLMs and revealed limitations in high-level planning, spatial reasoning, and physical inference. The paper highlights persistent errors in spatial orientation and dependency reasoning, even with chain-of-thought prompting. The authors position PhyBlock as a unified testbed to bridge vision-language understanding and real-world physical problem-solving.

**Rigorous and Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The novelty of the paper is significant, albeit not revolutionary. Previous works often emphasized passive understanding of physical environments or basic manipulation tasks. PhyBlock introduces a combination of hierarchical planning and explicit physical reasoning VQA, designed to be progressive. The emphasis on 3D block assembly is itself a relatively intuitive and interpretable task setting that highlights various aspects of embodied reasoning. The use of a physics simulator with attention to realistic 3D scenes, and AOV-based evaluation are valuable contributions. However, some prior benchmarks explored similar concepts, even if less progressively, so the key novelty lies in the specific combination and thoroughness of the framework.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance stems from its ability to identify key limitations in modern VLMs regarding physical understanding and planning. The benchmark exposes areas where VLMs still struggle, such as spatial dependency reasoning and long-horizon planning, even when prompted with chain-of-thought approaches. This helps direct research efforts towards developing models that can better incorporate physical priors. The comprehensive evaluation involving a large number of state-of-the-art models, both closed-source and open-source, makes the benchmark attractive and valuable to the community. Also, identifying that even Chain-of-Thought prompting helps only marginally hints at an even deeper need for true physical understanding and reasoning that VLMs can incorporate.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Well-Designed Benchmark:** The hierarchical structure and progressive difficulty levels are a major strength.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** Benchmarking a diverse set of VLMs reveals valuable insights into their capabilities and limitations.
    *   **Clear Error Analysis:** The categorization of error types (Euler errors, dependency errors, etc.) provides a useful diagnostic tool.
    *   **Physically Grounded:** Using a physics simulator adds realism and allows for evaluating models' ability to reason about dynamic interactions.
    *   **Clear Articulation of Limitations:** The paper acknowledges the limitations of the work and proposes concrete directions for future research (e.g., including VLA models, increasing viewpoint diversity).
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Scope:** While the focus on 3D block assembly provides a structured environment, it may be considered a specialized setting. The generalizability of the findings to more complex, unstructured real-world scenarios could be a concern.
    *   **Evaluation Setting:** The current evaluation still operates largely within a simulation. While physically grounded, the translation of these results to real-world robotic manipulation might not be direct. More direct comparisons to real robot setups would strengthen the paper.
    *   **Limited Action Space:** The actions are relatively high-level and don't include low-level motor controls. Future extensions involving integration with robot control policies will make the benchmark more practical.
    *   **Lack of Solutions/Mitigation Strategies:** The paper provides little insight in how to combat the found limitations. Although acknowledging is the first step, suggestions on what to tackle in the future would enhance the paper.

*   **Potential Influence:** PhyBlock has the potential to become a valuable resource for the embodied AI and robotics community. By providing a standardized benchmark and a set of diagnostic tools, it can help drive progress in developing more physically aware and capable VLMs. The paper's clear articulation of limitations and future directions will likely stimulate further research in this area.

**Justification for Score:**

Considering the factors above, I assign the paper a score of **8**. The PhyBlock benchmark has considerable novelty and significance as a comprehensive and rigorous evaluation framework for physical reasoning and planning in VLMs. While it has some limitations in terms of scope, evaluation setting, and action granularity, the paper makes a valuable contribution by identifying key bottlenecks in current models and proposing concrete directions for future research. The open-source nature of the benchmark and the thorough evaluation of a large number of models increase its potential for impact within the community. The weaknesses, although limiting, are also opportunities for future work.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.RO
- **Score**: 8/10

### ConfPO: Exploiting Policy Model Confidence for Critical Token Selection in Large Language Model Preference Optimization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.08712v1)
- **Authors**: Hee Suk Yoon, Eunseop Yoon, Mark A. Hasegawa-Johnson, Sungwoong Kim, Chang D. Yoo
- **Abstract**: We introduce ConfPO, a method for preference learning in Large Language Models (LLMs) that identifies and optimizes preference-critical tokens based solely on the training policy's confidence, without requiring any auxiliary models or compute. Unlike prior Direct Alignment Algorithms (DAAs) such as Direct Preference Optimization (DPO), which uniformly adjust all token probabilities regardless of their relevance to preference, ConfPO focuses optimization on the most impactful tokens. This targeted approach improves alignment quality while mitigating overoptimization (i.e., reward hacking) by using the KL divergence budget more efficiently. In contrast to recent token-level methods that rely on credit-assignment models or AI annotators, raising concerns about scalability and reliability, ConfPO is simple, lightweight, and model-free. Experimental results on challenging alignment benchmarks, including AlpacaEval 2 and Arena-Hard, demonstrate that ConfPO consistently outperforms uniform DAAs across various LLMs, delivering better alignment with zero additional computational overhead.
- **Summary**: The paper "ConfPO: Exploiting Policy Model Confidence for Critical Token Selection in Large Language Model Preference Optimization" introduces a novel method, ConfPO, for preference learning in large language models (LLMs). Unlike Direct Alignment Algorithms (DAAs) such as DPO, which uniformly adjust token probabilities, ConfPO focuses on optimizing only the tokens deemed most critical for preference alignment, identified by the training policy's own confidence scores. This targeted approach aims to improve alignment quality, mitigate overoptimization, and improve KL divergence budget usage. The paper demonstrates through experiments on AlpacaEval 2 and Arena-Hard benchmarks that ConfPO outperforms uniform DAAs across several LLMs without increasing computational overhead. The code is publicly available.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The key novelty lies in the selective token optimization guided by the policy model's internal confidence scores. The paper argues that not all tokens contribute equally to preference learning, and prioritizing low-confidence tokens (high surprisal) is more effective. While previous works have explored token-level reward signals, ConfPO distinguishes itself by deriving the selection criterion directly from the model's internal signals, maintaining computational efficiency comparable to standard DAAs.
*   **Significance:** The paper's significance lies in its potential to enhance alignment quality and mitigate overoptimization in LLMs, addressing two key challenges in the field. By reducing updates to unimportant tokens, the effective use of the KL divergence can improve overall result. The approach's simplicity and computational efficiency make it practical and scalable, potentially influencing future DAA research.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Concept:** The idea of focusing optimization on critical tokens identified by the model's own confidence is well-motivated and conceptually sound.
    *   **Experiments:** The experimental results on benchmark datasets show consistent performance improvements over existing DAAs. Ablations are provided to show it is not a random selection process that improves results.
    *   **Theoretical Justification:** The paper provides both empirical and theoretical analyses supporting the relationship between token confidence and its gradient.
    *   **Efficiency:** The method does not incur additional computational cost as opposed to other token-level reward schemes.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Scope of Evaluation:** While the paper presents results on AlpacaEval 2 and Arena-Hard, exploring a more diverse set of benchmarks, including those focusing on different aspects of alignment (e.g., safety, factual accuracy), would strengthen the conclusions. Also, the datasets used for training are based on scoring by a single model which could have an impact.
    *   **Limited Theoretical Depth:** While the theoretical rationale is provided, a more in-depth analysis on the impact of token selection on the loss function would clarify results more clearly.
    *   **Parameter sensitivity:** A clear study on parameter sensitivity could be provided to show which hyperparameters impact the selection process most directly.

Considering these strengths and weaknesses, the paper represents a solid advancement in the field. It offers a computationally efficient method, well-supported by experiments and theoretical underpinnings, to address a recognized challenge in LLM alignment.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Explainable Compliance Detection with Multi-Hop Natural Language Inference on Assurance Case Structure
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.08713v1)
- **Authors**: Fariz Ikhwantri, Dusica Marijan
- **Abstract**: Ensuring complex systems meet regulations typically requires checking the validity of assurance cases through a claim-argument-evidence framework. Some challenges in this process include the complicated nature of legal and technical texts, the need for model explanations, and limited access to assurance case data. We propose a compliance detection approach based on Natural Language Inference (NLI): EXplainable CompLiance detection with Argumentative Inference of Multi-hop reasoning (EXCLAIM). We formulate the claim-argument-evidence structure of an assurance case as a multi-hop inference for explainable and traceable compliance detection. We address the limited number of assurance cases by generating them using large language models (LLMs). We introduce metrics that measure the coverage and structural consistency. We demonstrate the effectiveness of the generated assurance case from GDPR requirements in a multi-hop inference task as a case study. Our results highlight the potential of NLI-based approaches in automating the regulatory compliance process.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper proposes a novel compliance detection approach called EXCLAIM (Explainable CompLiance detection with Argumentative Inference of Multi-hop reasoning), which leverages Natural Language Inference (NLI) and assurance case structures. It formulates the claim-argument-evidence (CAE) structure of an assurance case as a multi-hop inference problem, aiming to improve explainability and traceability in compliance detection.  The authors address the lack of assurance case data by generating synthetic data using large language models (LLMs) and introduce metrics for evaluating the quality of the generated data (coverage and structural consistency). A case study based on GDPR requirements demonstrates the potential of the approach.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The core idea of using NLI on assurance case structures to achieve explainable compliance detection has some novelty. Previous work has explored rule-based systems or text classification but haven't directly integrated multi-hop inference on the CAE structure in this specific manner, especially with a focus on explicit reasoning and explainability metrics tailored to assurance cases. The use of LLMs to *generate* assurance cases to tackle the data scarcity problem is a practical and potentially impactful contribution.  However, the combination of NLI and LLMs for compliance detection is not entirely new, so the degree of novelty needs to be critically assessed.

* **Significance:**  The paper addresses a crucial problem: ensuring compliance with complex regulations in a transparent and auditable way.  Automating this process with explainable AI can reduce costs and legal risks. The focus on explainability is especially important given the increasing scrutiny of AI systems in regulated domains.  The use of assurance cases as a structural representation provides a valuable framework for representing compliance arguments. The paper provides some practical insights on using LLMs to generate such cases, although the evaluation metrics need further refinement. The proposed methodology could be applied to various regulatory environments beyond GDPR.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly defines the challenges of compliance detection and the limitations of existing approaches.
    * **Well-Defined Methodology:** The EXCLAIM approach is clearly articulated, with a sound rationale for using NLI and assurance case structures.
    * **Practical Solution:** The use of LLMs to generate assurance cases addresses the data scarcity problem in a pragmatic way.
    * **Evaluation Metrics:** The proposed metrics (flat and structured) provide a starting point for assessing the quality of generated assurance cases, although these need further refinement and validation.
    * **Emphasis on Explainability:** The inclusion of post-hoc explanation methods and faithfulness metrics highlights the importance of model transparency.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Evaluation:** The evaluation relies primarily on synthetic data generated by LLMs. A more rigorous evaluation with real-world assurance case data would significantly strengthen the claims. The dependence on the quality of LLM-generated data remains a concern.
    * **Simplistic Metrics:** While the proposed metrics are a good start, they might not fully capture the logical validity or legal soundness of assurance cases. More sophisticated metrics or integration with formal verification methods might be necessary.  The "Graph Edit Distance" is a crude measure of the relationship.
    * **Limited Ablation Studies:** More ablation studies could be included to demonstrate the individual contribution of different components of the EXCLAIM approach.
    * **Clarity Regarding Implementation:** The implementation details (e.g., the specific architecture of the multi-hop NLI model) are not sufficiently detailed.
    * **Weak Claims of Improvements on LLM faithfulness:** The differences are small and not significant. More research will have to be done.

* **Potential Impact:** If the proposed approach can be scaled and validated with real-world data, it has the potential to significantly impact the field of compliance automation. The focus on explainability and traceability makes it particularly appealing for regulated industries.  The LLM-based assurance case generation could democratize access to structured compliance arguments.

**Justification:**

While the paper presents an interesting and potentially valuable approach, the current evaluation is limited and the novelty, while present, is not groundbreaking. The reliance on synthetic data and the simplistic nature of the structural metrics are major weaknesses.  The paper could be strengthened by including more real-world data, refining the evaluation metrics, and providing more detailed implementation details. The faithfulness metrics have a lot of challenges.

### Improved LLM Agents for Financial Document Question Answering
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.08726v1)
- **Authors**: Nelvin Tan, Zian Seng, Liang Zhang, Yu-Ching Shih, Dong Yang, Amol Salunkhe
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) have shown impressive capabilities on numerous natural language processing tasks. However, LLMs still struggle with numerical question answering for financial documents that include tabular and textual data. Recent works have showed the effectiveness of critic agents (i.e., self-correction) for this task given oracle labels. Building upon this framework, this paper examines the effectiveness of the traditional critic agent when oracle labels are not available, and show, through experiments, that this critic agent's performance deteriorates in this scenario. With this in mind, we present an improved critic agent, along with the calculator agent which outperforms the previous state-of-the-art approach (program-of-thought) and is safer. Furthermore, we investigate how our agents interact with each other, and how this interaction affects their performance.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates and aims to improve the numerical reasoning capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs) when applied to question answering tasks involving financial documents that contain both tabular and textual data.  The authors build upon a multi-agent framework using LLMs, particularly focusing on the effectiveness of critic agents (self-correction mechanisms). They find that, contrary to some recent research, the basic critic agent does *not* outperform Chain-of-Thought (CoT) prompting when oracle labels are unavailable. To address this, the paper introduces an improved critic agent inspired by the idea that LLMs can gauge their own confidence, as well as a novel calculator agent to enhance performance. The paper then examines interactions between these agents and demonstrates that the combined approach generally outperforms the program-of-thought (PoT) method (previous state-of-the-art for this task) and proposes the calculator agent as a safer alternative to the potentially dangerous executable Python code that PoT generates.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Definition and Relevance:** The paper tackles a relevant and challenging problem – numerical reasoning over tabular and textual data in financial documents. The practical applications in finance are clear.
    *   **Well-defined Experiments:**  The experimental setup is clearly described, making it easy to understand the evaluation process.
    *   **Analysis of existing approaches:** The authors pointed out the weakness of the existing critic agent and made a reasonable improvement.
    *   **Detailed Analysis:** The paper has a comprehensive analysis on the contribution of the critic agent by carefully examining its behavior given the model's confidence levels.
    *   **Practical Consideration (Safety):** The paper raises an important practical concern regarding the safety of the PoT approach, as it involves executing potentially dangerous code generated by an LLM. This is a crucial consideration for real-world deployment.
    *   **Reproducibility:** The authors provide clear details of their implementation, including a discussion of the LLMs used and a link to prompts used to construct the different agents and make it straightforward for readers to reproduce the main results.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited LLM Selection:** The paper only explores two LLMs. While they mention wanting to use the same LLM as a previous study that had access to oracle labels, it still only considers a small number of LLMs and one could question whether the findings truly hold up across a wider range of models.
    *   **Narrow Domain:** Both datasets used are from the financial domain. This limits the generalizability of the findings. Though the authors claim their focus is on numerical reasoning rather than financial expertise, the domain does influence the type of numerical reasoning required (e.g., understanding specific financial ratios or calculations).
    *   **Incremental Improvement:** The gains achieved by the proposed methods, while demonstrable, are somewhat incremental. While the combination of the improved critic and calculator agent provides the most significant improvement, the contributions of each of the agents may not be significant.
    *   **Lack of Ablation Study:** It would be helpful to see the ablation study to further validate the contributions of each agent.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

    *   The observation that the basic critic agent does *not* consistently improve over CoT without oracle labels is important, even if it agrees with a recent hypothesis of other works. Generalizing the findings for tabular and textual data is important since tabular data is different from textual data.
    *   The introduction of a confidence-aware critic agent and the calculator agent are novel contributions. The focus on a safe alternative to PoT is also significant.
    *   The analysis of the critic agent's behavior, particularly its tendency to sometimes worsen correct answers, is insightful.

*   **Potential Influence on the Field:**

    *   The paper could influence future research on multi-agent LLM systems for numerical reasoning, particularly in the financial domain.
    *   The safety arguments against PoT could lead to the adoption of safer alternatives like the calculator agent.

**Overall:**

The paper makes a useful contribution by demonstrating a safer alternative to PoT and by empirically validating that the traditional critic agent does not outperform the CoT approach when oracle labels are unavailable. The experiments are done well, and the analysis is detailed. The limitations regarding domain and LLM selection, however, should be addressed. Considering the combination of strengths and weaknesses, the novelty of the proposed improvement (critic+cal agent) is incremental, but it is significant because it has a real world application by safely improving the reasoning ability of LLMs with tabular and textual data.

### Breaking the ICE: Exploring promises and challenges of benchmarks for Inference Carbon & Energy estimation for LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.08727v1)
- **Authors**: Samarth Sikand, Rohit Mehra, Priyavanshi Pathania, Nikhil Bamby, Vibhu Saujanya Sharma, Vikrant Kaulgud, Sanjay Podder, Adam P. Burden
- **Abstract**: While Generative AI stands to be one of the fastest adopted technologies ever, studies have made evident that the usage of Large Language Models (LLMs) puts significant burden on energy grids and our environment. It may prove a hindrance to the Sustainability goals of any organization. A crucial step in any Sustainability strategy is monitoring or estimating the energy consumption of various components. While there exist multiple tools for monitoring energy consumption, there is a dearth of tools/frameworks for estimating the consumption or carbon emissions. Current drawbacks of both monitoring and estimation tools include high input data points, intrusive nature, high error margin, etc. We posit that leveraging emerging LLM benchmarks and related data points can help overcome aforementioned challenges while balancing accuracy of the emission estimations. To that extent, we discuss the challenges of current approaches and present our evolving framework, R-ICE, which estimates prompt level inference carbon emissions by leveraging existing state-of-the-art(SOTA) benchmark. This direction provides a more practical and non-intrusive way to enable emerging use-cases like dynamic LLM routing, carbon accounting, etc. Our promising validation results suggest that benchmark-based modelling holds great potential for inference emission estimation and warrants further exploration from the scientific community.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Breaking the ICE: Exploring promises and challenges of benchmarks for Inference Carbon & Energy estimation for LLMs" addresses the increasing environmental concerns associated with the widespread use of Large Language Models (LLMs). It argues that while monitoring energy consumption is important, accurately estimating the carbon emissions and energy usage specifically for *inference* is lacking. The paper proposes a framework called R-ICE (Regression-based Inference Carbon and Energy estimation) that leverages existing LLM benchmarks, particularly HELM-Efficiency, to estimate prompt-level inference carbon emissions. This approach aims to be less intrusive, more practical, and adaptable to organizations relying on LLM APIs without access to internal architectural details. The authors validate their framework against external benchmarks, showing promising results with a reasonable prediction error.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in its practical approach to estimating LLM inference carbon emissions by leveraging existing benchmarks. While the core idea of using regression models isn't groundbreaking, the application of this technique to benchmark data for carbon estimation in the context of resource-constrained environments (where model architecture data is unavailable) adds a level of novelty. The proposed method reduces the dependency on invasive monitoring tools or detailed architectural information. The paper leverages the HEALM framework to collect appropriate data for creating regression models to reduce data requirements.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a pressing and important issue: the environmental impact of LLMs. By providing a more accessible and practical estimation method, it can empower organizations to incorporate carbon considerations into their LLM deployment decisions, potentially leading to more sustainable AI practices. The proposed approach is significant as it addresses the specific challenge of estimating carbon emissions when using third-party LLM APIs, where internal details are unavailable. This fills a gap in existing tools that often require deeper access. The framework provides a path towards dynamic LLM routing based on carbon considerations and supports better carbon accounting.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Practicality:** The framework focuses on practical implementation and ease of use, aligning with the needs of many organizations using LLMs via APIs. The R-ICE method offers a less intrusive way to assess the environmental impact of LLM usage.
    *   **Leverages Existing Resources:** The paper effectively utilizes publicly available benchmarks, specifically HELM-Efficiency and LLM-perf, to build and validate its framework, reducing the need for custom data collection.
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies the limitations of current monitoring and estimation methods, motivating the need for a new approach.
    *   **Promising Validation:** The early validation results show a reasonable level of accuracy, demonstrating the potential of the framework.
    *   Clear description of architecture and framework implementation with appropriate diagram.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Validation Data:** The datasets size is still small. The validation relies on limited data points from HELM-Efficiency and LLM-perf, which could affect the generalizability of the results. A more comprehensive evaluation with a wider range of LLMs and real-world scenarios would strengthen the findings.
    *   **Simplifying Assumptions:** The framework relies on several simplifying assumptions, such as a fixed PUE, singleton batch inference, and limited context length. These assumptions might not hold in all real-world deployments, potentially affecting the accuracy of the estimates.
    *   **Dependency on Benchmark Quality:** The accuracy of R-ICE is directly tied to the quality and representativeness of the benchmark data. Any bias or limitations in the benchmarks will propagate into the emissions estimates.
    *   **Limited Evaluation metrics**: Use of more evaluation metrics could demonstrate robustness of the proposed method.
    *   There are some areas where the paper could have improved the clarity. For example, what is the difference between Regression-Modeling 2 and Regression-Modeling 3.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence organizations using LLMs to become more environmentally conscious. It could drive the adoption of more sustainable AI practices and stimulate further research into efficient LLM inference and carbon estimation methods. The proposed framework might inspire the development of similar tools and approaches in other domains of AI.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper offers a valuable contribution to the field by providing a practical method for estimating LLM inference carbon emissions in resource-constrained environments. While it has some limitations, the approach is novel and addresses a pressing issue. The validation results show promise, and the paper has the potential to influence organizations to adopt more sustainable AI practices.

### Unlocking the Potential of Large Language Models in the Nuclear Industry with Synthetic Data
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.08750v1)
- **Authors**: Muhammad Anwar, Daniel Lau, Mishca de Costa, Issam Hammad
- **Abstract**: The nuclear industry possesses a wealth of valuable information locked away in unstructured text data. This data, however, is not readily usable for advanced Large Language Model (LLM) applications that require clean, structured question-answer pairs for tasks like model training, fine-tuning, and evaluation. This paper explores how synthetic data generation can bridge this gap, enabling the development of robust LLMs for the nuclear domain. We discuss the challenges of data scarcity and privacy concerns inherent in the nuclear industry and how synthetic data provides a solution by transforming existing text data into usable Q&A pairs. This approach leverages LLMs to analyze text, extract key information, generate relevant questions, and evaluate the quality of the resulting synthetic dataset. By unlocking the potential of LLMs in the nuclear industry, synthetic data can pave the way for improved information retrieval, enhanced knowledge sharing, and more informed decision-making in this critical sector.
- **Summary**: **Summary:**

This paper explores the application of Large Language Models (LLMs) in the nuclear industry through synthetic data generation. It addresses the challenges of data scarcity and privacy concerns that hinder the use of real nuclear data for training and evaluating LLMs. The authors propose a pipeline that converts unstructured text from the "Essential CANDU" textbook into structured question-answer (Q&A) pairs using LLMs. This pipeline involves text chunking, summarization, concept extraction, embedding generation, clustering, and Q&A generation. The generated synthetic data is then evaluated for semantic diversity, document relevance, and question quality using various metrics and visualizations. The paper concludes by discussing future work and potential improvements to the proposed methodology.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in its application of a known technique (synthetic data generation using LLMs) to a specific, highly regulated domain: the nuclear industry, focusing on CANDU reactor technology. While the individual components of the pipeline (text chunking, summarization, embedding, clustering, Q&A generation) are not novel in themselves, the specific combination and adaptation to the unique challenges of the nuclear domain contribute to its relative novelty. The paper's strength is showing how this approach is specifically tailored to overcome the inherent restrictions present in the nuclear field. This focus on a niche application adds value.

**Significance:** The significance of the paper stems from the potential to unlock valuable information hidden within unstructured nuclear documents and to improve information retrieval, knowledge sharing, and decision-making in this sector. The stringent privacy regulations and data scarcity make traditional LLM training difficult, and this work presents a viable alternative to using sensitive data. The pipeline for generating Q&A pairs specifically targeting CANDU reactor technology is a significant contribution, as it can directly enable more effective use of LLMs within this industry. The paper's assessment of semantic diversity, document relevance, and question quality, provides a concrete methodology for others to follow and evaluate similar synthetic data generation efforts. One limitation lies in the relatively small dataset used to validate the pipeline.

**Weaknesses:**

*   The paper focuses primarily on describing the pipeline and presenting initial results. The evaluation section presents a good start to measuring the quality of generated pairs, but the dataset used for validation (i.e., the "Essential CANDU" textbook) is limited. Testing the robustness of LLMs trained using the generated data is very limited. This would strengthen the claims regarding its effectiveness.
*   The analysis in this paper could be more rigorous, possibly with human expert assessment of the generated Q\&A pairs. Although evaluation techniques are discussed, they could be more extensive.
*   The discussion on future work is generic. The paper needs to provide specific guidance for further development.

**Strengths:**

*   The paper provides a clear and detailed description of the synthetic data generation pipeline, including specific techniques and tools used.
*   It addresses a relevant and significant problem in the nuclear industry, where data scarcity and privacy concerns are major obstacles.
*   The evaluation section presents a good preliminary assessment of the generated Q\&A pairs, using both quantitative metrics and visualizations.
*   It emphasizes the contextualization and structuring of information, important for technical domains.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper demonstrates a practically relevant application of existing LLM techniques to the problem of data scarcity in the nuclear industry. The detailed description of the pipeline and preliminary evaluation are commendable. However, it needs additional validation that involves evaluating the performance of actual LLMs trained with the synthetic data. Also, additional human expert assessment of the generated pairs could be a great addition. It is a moderately novel approach, with limited impact outside this niche area.

### Enhancing Accuracy and Maintainability in Nuclear Plant Data Retrieval: A Function-Calling LLM Approach Over NL-to-SQL
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.08757v1)
- **Authors**: Mishca de Costa, Muhammad Anwar, Dave Mercier, Mark Randall, Issam Hammad
- **Abstract**: Retrieving operational data from nuclear power plants requires exceptional accuracy and transparency due to the criticality of the decisions it supports. Traditionally, natural language to SQL (NL-to-SQL) approaches have been explored for querying such data. While NL-to-SQL promises ease of use, it poses significant risks: end-users cannot easily validate generated SQL queries, and legacy nuclear plant databases -- often complex and poorly structured -- complicate query generation due to decades of incremental modifications. These challenges increase the likelihood of inaccuracies and reduce trust in the approach. In this work, we propose an alternative paradigm: leveraging function-calling large language models (LLMs) to address these challenges. Instead of directly generating SQL queries, we define a set of pre-approved, purpose-specific functions representing common use cases. Queries are processed by invoking these functions, which encapsulate validated SQL logic. This hybrid approach mitigates the risks associated with direct NL-to-SQL translations by ensuring that SQL queries are reviewed and optimized by experts before deployment. While this strategy introduces the upfront cost of developing and maintaining the function library, we demonstrate how NL-to-SQL tools can assist in the initial generation of function code, allowing experts to focus on validation rather than creation. Our study includes a performance comparison between direct NL-to-SQL generation and the proposed function-based approach, highlighting improvements in accuracy and maintainability. This work underscores the importance of balancing user accessibility with operational safety and provides a novel, actionable framework for robust data retrieval in critical systems.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenges of using Natural Language to SQL (NL-to-SQL) approaches for retrieving operational data from nuclear power plant databases. It argues that direct NL-to-SQL translation introduces risks due to the complexity and legacy nature of these databases and the difficulty in validating generated SQL queries. The paper proposes an alternative function-calling LLM approach where instead of generating SQL directly, the LLM calls pre-approved, purpose-specific functions that encapsulate validated SQL logic. This hybrid approach aims to improve accuracy, transparency, and maintainability.  The paper outlines the system's architecture, addresses challenges like technical jargon and structured output, and compares the proposed method against a more traditional NL-to-SQL approach. Evaluation metrics include LLM-computed metrics and human-assessed correctness.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The *core* idea of using function-calling LLMs over direct NL-to-SQL for critical data retrieval isn't entirely novel in the broader NLP landscape.  Function calling as a concept is already well established. However, the *application* of this approach to the *specific domain* of nuclear power plant operational data retrieval offers *some* novelty.  The constraints imposed by the critical nature of the data and the legacy database structures provide a unique context. The specific architectural decisions, particularly regarding multi-agent workflow with retry and error handling, are somewhat more original, although they are built on established techniques.  The focus on expert validation of SQL functions is a strong emphasis that improves trustworthiness.

**Significance:**  The significance lies primarily in its potential practical impact. If the proposed approach demonstrably improves accuracy and reduces the risk associated with data retrieval in nuclear power plants, it could have considerable value.  The paper emphasizes maintainability, a critical aspect in long-lived industrial systems.  However, the current paper lacks extensive quantitative results. While it mentions that human evaluators favor the function-calling approach, the magnitude of the improvement needs further investigation.  The inclusion of information on the specific database schema, queries used for evaluation, and the number of human experts providing assessment would improve the evaluation.

**Strengths:**

*   **Addresses a real-world problem:** The paper tackles a tangible challenge with significant implications for safety and operational efficiency in a critical infrastructure domain.
*   **Clear articulation of the problem:** The limitations of NL-to-SQL in this specific context are clearly explained.
*   **Well-structured methodology:** The proposed function-calling approach and the system architecture are logically presented.
*   **Emphasis on safety and maintainability:** These are crucial considerations in the target domain.
*   **Addresses relevant challenges:** It considers the complexities of technical jargon and structured output, and addresses these concerns through solutions such as the JSON output and Pydantic models.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Empirical Validation:** The evaluation lacks quantitative rigor. While human evaluation favors the function calling approach, a larger-scale and more detailed comparison of accuracy, precision, recall, and F1-score against ground truth across a diverse query set would be much stronger. The paper references Figure 5 as providing evidence, yet it is difficult to interpret the results from this graph without further explanation.
*   **Lack of Specific Implementation Details:** Information about the specific functions created, the database schema, and the NL-to-SQL tools used is absent. This makes it difficult to assess the practicality and transferability of the approach. It is unclear if there were any specific models used to generate SQL functions or whether this was accomplished through the function-calling process.
*   **Limited Novelty in Core Concept:** The novelty lies mainly in the application rather than the core approach itself.
*   **Unclear Error Analysis:** The paper does not give an in-depth analysis of the types of errors made by each approach.  Understanding the specific failure modes is critical for judging the suitability of the function-calling method.
*   **Scalability Concerns:** The function-calling approach can be difficult to scale. Adding new functions to cater to different types of user questions can become tedious.

**Potential Influence:**

If the proposed approach can be demonstrably superior to direct NL-to-SQL in terms of accuracy and safety, it could influence how data retrieval systems are designed in critical infrastructure domains beyond nuclear power. It offers a more rigorous solution for complex systems that require increased governance and transparency.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper tackles a problem with real-world impact using an approach that offers some degree of originality in application. The methodology is well-structured, but the empirical validation is weak, and there is a lack of specific implementation details. While this paper is helpful, these factors significantly limit the current value.

### AraReasoner: Evaluating Reasoning-Based LLMs for Arabic NLP
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.08768v1)
- **Authors**: Ahmed Hasanaath, Aisha Alansari, Ahmed Ashraf, Chafik Salmane, Hamzah Luqman, Saad Ezzini
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) have shown remarkable progress in reasoning abilities and general natural language processing (NLP) tasks, yet their performance on Arabic data, characterized by rich morphology, diverse dialects, and complex script, remains underexplored. This paper presents a comprehensive benchmarking study of multiple reasoning-focused LLMs, with a special emphasis on the newly introduced DeepSeek models, across a suite of fifteen Arabic NLP tasks. We experiment with various strategies, including zero-shot, few-shot, and fine-tuning. This allows us to systematically evaluate performance on datasets covering a range of applications to examine their capacity for linguistic reasoning under different levels of complexity. Our experiments reveal several key findings. First, carefully selecting just three in-context examples delivers an average uplift of over 13 F1 points on classification tasks-boosting sentiment analysis from 35.3% to 87.5% and paraphrase detection from 56.1% to 87.0%. Second, reasoning-focused DeepSeek architectures outperform a strong GPT o4-mini baseline by an average of 12 F1 points on complex inference tasks in the zero-shot setting. Third, LoRA-based fine-tuning yields up to an additional 8 points in F1 and BLEU compared to equivalent increases in model scale. The code is available at https://anonymous.4open.science/r/AraReasoner41299
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces AraReasoner, a benchmark for evaluating the reasoning abilities of large language models (LLMs) on Arabic natural language processing (NLP) tasks. It presents a comprehensive study across fifteen diverse Arabic NLP tasks, categorized into sentence classification, natural language generation, and linguistic analysis. The authors experiment with zero-shot, few-shot, and fine-tuning strategies using a range of LLMs, including DeepSeek models, Qwen-based models, and OpenAI's GPT models. The study analyzes the impact of different prompting strategies, model sizes, architectures, and training techniques on performance across these tasks. Key findings include the substantial performance gains achieved through careful selection of in-context examples, the competitive performance of reasoning-focused DeepSeek architectures, and the effectiveness of LoRA-based fine-tuning for further performance improvements. The AraReasoner benchmark and the accompanying analysis aim to address the underrepresentation of Arabic in NLP research and provide insights into the potential and limitations of LLMs in handling the complexities of the Arabic language.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in creating and systematically evaluating a diverse benchmark specifically for Arabic NLP, focusing on reasoning capabilities. While prior studies have explored LLMs for Arabic, AraReasoner's focus on reasoning, the inclusion of newer architectures like DeepSeek, and the breadth of tasks differentiate it from existing work. However, many existing Arabic NLP tasks are already available, meaning the novelty resides more in the **combination and systematic evaluation** rather than creation of totally new tasks.

*   **Significance:** The significance of the paper stems from its potential to accelerate research in Arabic NLP and promote the development of LLMs better suited to the unique challenges of the Arabic language, which are detailed in the paper, like rich morphology, multiple dialects, and non-Latin script. The benchmarking results provide valuable insights for practitioners and researchers in terms of model selection, prompting strategies, and fine-tuning approaches. The exploration of different LLM architectures and training techniques contributes to a better understanding of their effectiveness in handling the complexities of Arabic.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive evaluation:** The paper covers a wide range of tasks and models, providing a relatively thorough assessment of LLMs for Arabic NLP.
    *   **Detailed analysis:** The authors delve into the impact of various factors, such as prompting strategies, model size, and architecture, on performance.
    *   **Practical insights:** The findings offer actionable recommendations for model selection, prompting, and fine-tuning.
    *   **Addresses a gap:** The paper explicitly acknowledges and attempts to mitigate the underrepresentation of Arabic in NLP research.
    *   **Clear methodology:** The experimental setup and evaluation metrics are clearly defined, making the results reproducible.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited availability of resources:** The code is available but labelled anonymous so it is difficult to immediately assess its usability and extendability.
    *   **Prompt selection:** Using a single LLM (DeepSeek R1-Q14B) for prompt selection introduces potential bias, though this is standard practice.
    *   **Limited Model Architectures:** Though a good start, a lot of model architectures, from big tech to smaller companies, were not included in the benchmarks. This limits its comparison scope.
    *   **Limited data split details:** While data split is mentioned, further details could have been supplied, especially for any datasets where data split was not the default for official splits.

*   **Potential Impact:** The paper can contribute to the development of better Arabic NLP models by providing a clear evaluation framework and practical guidelines. It also underscores the importance of continued research efforts to address the unique challenges of the language and close the performance gap with other more high-resource languages. The work will likely influence researchers in the field by establishing a standardized evaluation procedure and setting a performance baseline.

*   **Justification for Score:** The paper makes a valuable contribution by presenting a comprehensive benchmark for reasoning-based LLMs on Arabic NLP tasks. The systematic evaluation, detailed analysis, and practical insights will be beneficial for researchers and practitioners in the field. While the AraReasoner benchmark and accompanying evaluations are useful additions to Arabic NLP, the evaluation of Arabic NLP and reasoning is a space that has already been explored by past research. The selection of datasets and LLMs are not always exhaustive (not all available models for Arabic NLP or existing datasets have been used) which may somewhat limit the generalizability of the results. For these reasons, the paper is not assigned a particularly high novelty and significance score.

### Paths to Causality: Finding Informative Subgraphs Within Knowledge Graphs for Knowledge-Based Causal Discovery
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.08771v1)
- **Authors**: Yuni Susanti, Michael Färber
- **Abstract**: Inferring causal relationships between variable pairs is crucial for understanding multivariate interactions in complex systems. Knowledge-based causal discovery -- which involves inferring causal relationships by reasoning over the metadata of variables (e.g., names or textual context) -- offers a compelling alternative to traditional methods that rely on observational data. However, existing methods using Large Language Models (LLMs) often produce unstable and inconsistent results, compromising their reliability for causal inference. To address this, we introduce a novel approach that integrates Knowledge Graphs (KGs) with LLMs to enhance knowledge-based causal discovery. Our approach identifies informative metapath-based subgraphs within KGs and further refines the selection of these subgraphs using Learning-to-Rank-based models. The top-ranked subgraphs are then incorporated into zero-shot prompts, improving the effectiveness of LLMs in inferring the causal relationship. Extensive experiments on biomedical and open-domain datasets demonstrate that our method outperforms most baselines by up to 44.4 points in F1 scores, evaluated across diverse LLMs and KGs. Our code and datasets are available on GitHub: https://github.com/susantiyuni/path-to-causality
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Paths to Causality: Finding Informative Subgraphs Within Knowledge Graphs for Knowledge-Based Causal Discovery":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenge of inferring causal relationships between variable pairs using knowledge-based causal discovery, particularly by leveraging Large Language Models (LLMs) and Knowledge Graphs (KGs). The authors propose a novel approach that integrates informative metapath-based subgraphs from KGs with LLMs to improve the accuracy and reliability of causal inference. The method consists of three modules: (1) Subgraph Relevancy Estimation, which uses LLMs to rank subgraphs based on their informativeness; (2) Subgraph Ranker, which trains specialized ranking models to refine subgraph selection; and (3) Knowledge-Based Causal Discovery, which incorporates top-ranked subgraphs into zero-shot prompts for LLMs. The authors demonstrate the effectiveness of their approach through experiments on biomedical and open-domain datasets, showing significant performance improvements compared to baseline methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

*   **Strength:** The core novelty lies in the specific combination of techniques: a Learning-to-Rank approach *specifically tailored* to select subgraphs for *knowledge-based causal discovery* when using LLMs.  Previous work has used KGs with LLMs for question answering and dialogue, and some has explored LLMs for causal discovery. However, the explicit ranking of KG subgraphs to enhance *zero-shot* causal reasoning within LLMs, coupled with the systematic comparison of different ranking methods, constitutes a significant contribution.
*   **Weakness:** Some components are based on existing concepts.  Metapath-based reasoning in KGs and RAG techniques are well-established. The use of LLMs for some level of causal reasoning is also not entirely new. The novelty depends on the *specific combination* and application, rather than being a completely groundbreaking method.
* **Overall:** The paper's strength lies in the methodical approach to integrating KGs with LLMs for causal discovery. It moves beyond simply retrieving information from KGs; it actively ranks and selects subgraphs to optimize the prompt for the LLM, this is a key differentiator.

**Significance:**

*   **Strength:** The paper addresses a critical problem: the instability and inconsistency of LLMs in causal reasoning, particularly in sensitive domains like healthcare. The proposed approach has the potential to improve the reliability of LLM-based causal discovery, making it a more trustworthy tool for tasks such as drug repurposing, side-effect prediction, and understanding disease mechanisms. The performance gains are substantial, showing that the approach effectively leverages structured knowledge to enhance LLM reasoning.
*   **Strength:** The comparative analysis is comprehensive, considering multiple LLMs, KGs, and subgraph ranking methods.  The experiments on both biomedical and open-domain datasets demonstrate the generalizability of the approach. The comparison with conventional statistical causal discovery is also valuable.
*   **Weakness:**  The paper focuses on *pairwise* causal discovery. While this is a fundamental step, it doesn't address the more complex scenario of *causal graph discovery* involving multiple interconnected variables.  The reliance on zero-shot prompting might limit the model's ability to capture nuanced causal relationships that require fine-tuning.
*   **Weakness:** The complexity of the approach might be a barrier to adoption. While the authors provide code, the need to train subgraph ranking models and carefully select metapaths requires significant expertise. The benefit over the baseline, while statistically significant, may not be large enough to justify the added complexity in a practical setting.

**Justification:**

The paper presents a significant advance in leveraging KGs to improve the accuracy and robustness of LLM-based causal discovery. While relying on established components (RAG, metapaths), the paper proposes a novel methodology by introducing learning to rank methods for selecting relevant subgraphs to improve LLM prompting for causal inference. This provides more reliability and a zero-shot approach that bypasses the limitations of traditional approaches. The comprehensive experiments and the detailed comparison with baselines support the claims. However, it doesn't achieve the highest score as it builds on pre-existing concepts, focuses solely on pairwise relationships, and carries significant complexity that may limit its broader application and adoption.

### Flow Diverse and Efficient: Learning Momentum Flow Matching via Stochastic Velocity Field Sampling
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.08796v1)
- **Authors**: Zhiyuan Ma, Ruixun Liu, Sixian Liu, Jianjun Li, Bowen Zhou
- **Abstract**: Recently, the rectified flow (RF) has emerged as the new state-of-the-art among flow-based diffusion models due to its high efficiency advantage in straight path sampling, especially with the amazing images generated by a series of RF models such as Flux 1.0 and SD 3.0. Although a straight-line connection between the noisy and natural data distributions is intuitive, fast, and easy to optimize, it still inevitably leads to: 1) Diversity concerns, which arise since straight-line paths only cover a fairly restricted sampling space. 2) Multi-scale noise modeling concerns, since the straight line flow only needs to optimize the constant velocity field $\bm v$ between the two distributions $\bm\pi_0$ and $\bm\pi_1$. In this work, we present Discretized-RF, a new family of rectified flow (also called momentum flow models since they refer to the previous velocity component and the random velocity component in each diffusion step), which discretizes the straight path into a series of variable velocity field sub-paths (namely ``momentum fields'') to expand the search space, especially when close to the distribution $p_\text{noise}$. Different from the previous case where noise is directly superimposed on $\bm x$, we introduce noise on the velocity $\bm v$ of the sub-path to change its direction in order to improve the diversity and multi-scale noise modeling abilities. Experimental results on several representative datasets demonstrate that learning momentum flow matching by sampling random velocity fields will produce trajectories that are both diverse and efficient, and can consistently generate high-quality and diverse results. Code is available at https://github.com/liuruixun/momentum-fm.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Discretized-RF, a novel approach to rectified flow (RF) based generative models, aimed at improving the trade-off between sampling efficiency and diversity. The method discretizes the straight path of traditional RF into a series of variable velocity field sub-paths ("momentum fields"). This allows for the introduction of noise along the velocity vector, which enhances the exploration of the sampling space and facilitates multi-scale noise modeling.  The authors argue that this momentum flow matching improves both diversity and retains the efficiency of RF.  Experiments on CIFAR-10 and CelebA-HQ suggest that the proposed method can achieve competitive or superior FID scores with fewer denoising steps compared to standard RF.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Addressing a Clear Limitation:** The paper tackles a well-known limitation of RF models: their lack of diversity due to the constrained straight-line trajectory. This is a significant and relevant problem in the field of generative modeling.
    *   **Intuitive and Plausible Approach:** The idea of discretizing the RF trajectory and introducing momentum-based velocity fluctuations is intuitive and offers a conceptually sound way to address the diversity problem.
    *   **Experimental Validation:** The paper provides experimental results that support the claim of improved diversity and comparable efficiency. The comparison with standard RF on CIFAR-10 and CelebA-HQ provides evidence for the method's effectiveness. The ablation studies exploring the influence of parameters like N and gamma show how the algorithm can be fine-tuned.
    *   **Code Availability:** The availability of the code allows for reproducibility and further exploration of the proposed method by the research community.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Incremental Novelty:** While the approach is sound, the novelty is somewhat incremental. It's essentially an extension of RF that incorporates elements reminiscent of diffusion models (specifically, the introduction of noise and momentum).  It does not revolutionize the flow-based methods.
    *   **Limited Evaluation:** The experimental results are limited to relatively small datasets (CIFAR-10, CelebA-HQ). The performance on larger, more complex datasets (e.g., ImageNet at higher resolutions) would provide a stronger validation.
    *   **Lack of Theoretical Depth:** The paper primarily focuses on the algorithmic aspect. More theoretical analysis regarding the optimality of the learned trajectories, the convergence properties, and guarantees on diversity would strengthen the work.  While the paper provides motivation based on Optimal Transport, it doesn't offer a significant theoretical contribution in that area.
    *   **Writing Clarity:** Certain parts of the paper could be improved in terms of clarity and organization. For example, the detailed discussion of the forward/reverse processes and the algorithms may seem slightly convoluted.
    *   **Marginal Gains:** While the quantitative results showcase an improvement, the gains may not be significant enough to justify the increased complexity, especially considering the results on CIFAR-10.

*   **Significance:**

    *   The paper's significance lies in its attempt to bridge the gap between the efficiency of RF and the diversity of diffusion models. This is a worthwhile pursuit that can potentially lead to more practical and versatile generative models.
    *   The momentum flow matching technique can be a useful tool for researchers working on flow-based models, providing a way to inject controlled stochasticity and improve sample quality.
    *   The paper can serve as a starting point for further research on adaptive flow trajectories and more sophisticated techniques for balancing diversity and efficiency in generative modeling.

**Justification:**

The paper presents a reasonable, experimentally validated approach to address a recognized weakness of RF models. The momentum flow matching technique is conceptually sound and provides a practical way to improve diversity without sacrificing too much efficiency. However, the novelty is incremental, the experimental evaluation could be more comprehensive, and the theoretical analysis is somewhat limited. For those reasons, it's a valuable extension to existing methods, but the overall contribution isn't a fundamental shift or paradigm-changing discovery. The contribution is more applied, focused on an important engineering improvement rather than a foundational breakthrough.
